| conc | 9 | Housing segregation as American original sin. The show explores NIMBYism, institutional racism, the difference between personal decency and political courage, the destruction wrought by good intentions facing organized resistance. Wasicsko's arc is about the cost of doing right in a system that punishes it. Simon's most didactic work is also his most morally complex. |
| drive | 7 | The pace is deliberate—this is David Simon, not Aaron Sorkin. Each episode advances the timeline and the stakes, but momentum builds through understanding rather than acceleration. Audiences seeking thrills will be bored; audiences seeking civic education dramatized will be riveted. The drive is intellectual as much as emotional. |
| resol | 9 | A complete miniseries: Wasicsko's rise and fall, the housing built, the resistance fading, the costs tallied. The ending includes the real Wasicsko's fate—a gut-punch that contextualizes everything. Simon knows how to conclude: with truth rather than triumph. Among HBO's finest miniseries. |

| char | 7 | Colin O'Brien as Edward carries the show with a performance that communicates overwhelming pressure without melodramatic acting. The adult cast creates distinct grieving styles, though the ensemble size limits individual development. The performances are respectful of the material without being subdued. |
| world | 6.5 | The series creates a sense of community among the bereaved, who find themselves connected by the same tragedy. The memorial services, the support groups, the particular rituals that emerge around mass death are rendered with attention to what grief actually looks like. |
| cine | 6 | The visual approach is understated—handheld cameras in close spaces, natural light, an observational style that doesn't call attention to itself. The direction serves the actors rather than overwhelming them with technique. |
| spect | 2 | There is no spectacle; the crash is not shown, the grief is interior. The show's visual interest is in faces and reactions, in the spaces where people wait for feelings they can't process. This is appropriate to the material but limits visual engagement. |
| conc | 7 | The series engages seriously with survivor's guilt, complicated grief, and the way tragedy creates community among strangers. Edward's status as sole survivor is treated as a burden rather than a miracle. The show resists easy redemption narratives. |
| drive | 6 | Grief television moves slowly by nature, and the multiple storylines require patience. Edward's story provides momentum, but the checks on other grieving families slow the pace. The structure serves the theme—grief isn't linear—but tests viewer attention. |
| resol | 6.5 | The ending provides emotional closure without easy resolution. Edward doesn't heal in any simple sense, and the other stories reach stopping points rather than conclusions. The show understands that some griefs don't resolve; they become part of the fabric of ongoing life. |

| char | 9 | Kate Winslet's Mildred is one of television's great performances—a woman whose strength is also her blindness. Evan Rachel Wood as Veda is terrifying: privilege weaponized against the mother who provides it. The supporting cast—Guy Pearce, James Le Gros, Melissa Leo—creates a complete world. Every performance is calibrated, period-appropriate, emotionally true. |
| world | 8 | 1930s Southern California rendered in forensic detail: the interiors, the restaurants, the social hierarchies, the Depression's effect on different classes. The world is Mildred's creation—her restaurants, her home, her relationships—and watching it built is witnessing labor translated into identity. The production design is museum-quality. |
| cine | 8 | Todd Haynes' direction is painterly. The color palette is saturated, almost Technicolor, contrasting with the story's emotional darkness. Every frame looks composed—the restaurant openings, the beach houses, the confrontation scenes. The mini-series format allows visual development uncommon in television. |
| spect | 6 | Period spectacle: restaurant designs, fashion, automobiles, architecture. The spectacle of class aspiration rendered visible—Mildred's restaurants as statements, her home as testament to success. This isn't action spectacle; it's material culture as character. Subtle but effective. |
| conc | 8 | The show explores mother-daughter dynamics with novelistic depth: the daughter who mirrors you, the daughter who rejects you. Class anxiety runs throughout—Mildred's success can never erase her working-class origin in Veda's eyes. The Depression is context for questions about survival, ambition, the costs of success. Rich material handled with intelligence. |
| drive | 7 | Five episodes, each feature-length. The pace is deliberate—this is a character study, not a thriller. Narrative momentum builds through relationship dynamics rather than plot mechanics. Some viewers will find it slow; those who appreciate character exploration will find it rewarding. |
| resol | 9 | A complete miniseries with devastating resolution. Veda's betrayal is inevitable; Mildred's blindness continues until it can't. The ending collapses Mildred's carefully constructed world, forcing acknowledgment of what she's refused to see. The resolution is tragic but earned—a perfect adaptation of Cain's novel. |

| char | 7 | Din Djarin is stoic, principled, emotionally constipated—and Pedro Pascal conveys all of it through body language and voice. Grogu (Baby Yoda) is a merchandising phenomenon and genuinely affecting character. The supporting cast rotates: Carl Weathers, Gina Carano, Giancarlo Esposito, Katee Sackhoff. Quality varies, but the core relationship—Mandalorian and foundling—anchors… |
| world | 8 | This is Star Wars as lived-in universe: remote planets, obscure cultures, the remnants of Empire and the chaos of the New Republic. The show expands Star Wars without breaking it—Mandalorian lore, Jedi mythology, the politics of a post-Imperial galaxy. Jon Favreau understands that world-building is texture: strange creatures, unfamiliar customs, languages we don't speak. |
| cine | 8 | The Volume—Disney's LED wall technology—creates immersive environments without green screen artifacts. The show looks unlike anything on television: cinematic scope, practical effects blended with digital backgrounds. Western compositions: wide shots, lone figures against landscapes, showdown lighting. This is TV that looks like film. |
| spect | 9 | Star Wars delivered weekly: space battles, creature effects, lightsaber duels, epic landscapes. The budget is bottomless, and it shows. Each episode features at least one sequence that would be a film highlight. The spectacle serves story—Grogu's Force powers, the Dark Trooper assault, the spiders of Torment—but spectacle is essential to the experience. |
| conc | 6 | The show explores the Mandalorian creed: honor, foundlings, tribal identity. It asks what it means to belong to something larger than yourself. But these themes play second fiddle to adventure. This is popcorn entertainment elevated by execution, not philosophical television disguised as action. |
| drive | 7 | Episodic with serialized elements. Each season has a quest structure: deliver the child, find a Jedi, save a planet. The drive is consistent but not relentless. Some episodes feel like diversions; others are essential. The show is comfortable being a Western serial—some adventures matter to the arc, some don't. Patient viewers will appreciate the pacing; others may find it… |
| resol | 6 | Ongoing. The show has no ending yet—each season resolves certain elements while opening others. The Mandalorian Creed is tested, the relationship with Grogu evolves, the galaxy remains in flux. The series serves Disney's larger Star Wars plans, which means conclusions are perpetually deferred. Quality remains high, but resolution remains future-tense. |

| char | 7 | The lead performance carries the show—a detective whose gift is also a burden, who sees violence he can't unsee. The portrayal captures both competence and weariness effectively. Supporting characters are functional: the partner who grounds the lead, the superior who creates obstacles, the victims whose deaths drive the investigation. Adequate but not memorable. |
| world | 6.5 | The show creates a recognizable version of Korean policing: procedural details, institutional dynamics, the particular pressures of detective work. The crime scenes are the most developed spaces—each one conveying the violence that occurred without excessive illustration. The broader world remains underdeveloped; we see what's necessary for the investigation and little else. |
| cine | 7 | Cross uses visual language effectively: crime scenes are shot with clinical distance that gradually warms as the detective reads them, the present giving way to implied past. The palette is desaturated, appropriate to the material. Key sequences—a nighttime pursuit through narrow streets, a confrontation in an abandoned building—are staged with skill. |
| spect | 4 | Crime thrillers don't generally provide spectacle, and Cross follows that pattern. There are chases and confrontations, but the show's visual interest lies in observation rather than action. The spectacle is psychological—watching a mind work through evidence—rather than physical. |
| conc | 6.5 | The show engages with questions about violence and its aftermath, about the cost of seeing too clearly. The detective's talent is framed as both gift and curse, and the toll it takes on his psychology is part of the story. The conspiracy finale diminishes these themes by providing external explanations for what worked better as character study. |
| drive | 7 | The episodic structure—discovering new cases, seeing connections, pursuing leads—provides consistent momentum. The eight-episode count prevents padding; every scene serves either the immediate investigation or the larger arc. The pacing is one of the show's strengths, moving forward with purpose even when the destination proves disappointing. |
| resol | 6 | The finale resolves the central mystery but requires accepting conveniences that earlier episodes didn't need. The explanation for the detective's gift is a letdown, and the conspiracy reveal feels small compared to the crimes that preceded it. The emotional resolutions for the detective work better than the plot mechanics, but the overall landing is bumpier than the flight. |

The supernatural effects are competent without being impressive. The show looks like what it is—a well-funded television romance, not a cinematic experience. |
| spect | 4 | The spectacle here is romantic rather than action-oriented. There are some supernatural effects—the demon's powers manifest visually when he has them—but the visual interest is in faces and chemistry, not in anything the production builds or destroys. |
| conc | 6 | The show uses the demon's immortality to gesture at themes about love and loneliness, about whether someone who can live forever would want to without the person they love. These ideas are present but not deeply explored; the format favors romantic resolution over philosophical inquiry. The themes serve the romance rather than vice versa. |
| drive | 7.5 | Rom-com plotting is formulaic but effective when done well, and My Demon does it well. Each episode advances the relationship through obstacles that feel imposed rather than contrived—family politics, supernatural threats, the demon's own past. The sixteen-episode arc builds naturally toward resolution. |
| resol | 7 | The conclusion delivers what it promises: the couple overcomes their obstacles, the demon makes the choice the audience expects, and the ending provides the emotional satisfaction that the genre requires. The fantasy elements are resolved in a way that allows for the expected happily-ever-after without feeling like a cheat. |

| char | 7 | Chloë Grace Moretz carries the show as Flynne—smart, distrustful, adapting to impossible information. Gary Carr'sWilf provides the future's perspective. The split between Flynne's rural present and Wilf's decadent future creates contrast without confusion. The villains are corporate without being boring. |
| world | 8 | Gibson's stub concept: alternate timelines that can be accessed and influenced. The future has developed the tech; the past becomes a battleground. The rural 2032 is economically depressed American South; the 2099 London is oligarchic excess. The rules are explained without drowning in exposition. |
| cine | 7 | Two distinct visual palettes: warm, saturated rural present versus cold, blue future urbanism. The peripheral sequences are seamless—you see Flynne's consciousness in a future body. The action is coherent; Gibson's concepts are visualized. |
| spect | 6 | The spectacle is conceptual: seeing through distant eyes, timelines branching and merging. The 2099 London has production design budget—hovercraft, drones, AR overlays. But the show prioritizes noir atmosphere over constant effects. |
| conc | 8 | Stub theory, timeline manipulation, corporate warfare across decades. The show explores what happens when the future treats the past as a game—not just ethically, but technically. Multiple timelines, multiple selves, causality loops. Gibson's ideas translated to screen without simplification. |
| drive | 7.5 | The conspiracy pulls Flynne deeper each episode. The stakes scale: from personal survival to timeline stability. The future characters need something from Flynne's past; she needs to survive both. The mystery unfolds at Gibson pace: deliberate, then explosive. |
| resol | 5 | The show was cancelled after season 1. The finale sets up season 2 that will never come. The immediate plot resolves but larger arcs remain open. As an incomplete work, it's Gibson at his most accessible, but frustration is unavoidable. |

| char | 8.5 | Jeff Bridges is the show—seventy, trained, tired, dangerous. Every movement shows age; every fight is a calculation of remaining energy. John Lithgow as his pursuer creates mutual respect between enemies. Amy Brenneman's Zoe provides civilian vulnerability. Alia Shawkat's agent represents institutional justice versus personal redemption. This is acting as embodied character. |
| world | 7.5 | The Afghanistan of the 1980s: CIA proxy wars, mujahideen alliances, decisions that created future enemies. The present—surveillance state, aging intelligence infrastructure, the cost of buried secrets. The show understands that spy craft is relationships and consequences. |
| cine | 7.5 | The action is grounded—Chase fights like an old man who knows he's old. Close-quarters combat shows exhaustion. The flashbacks have period texture without nostalgia. The present is paranoia rendered in shadows and security cameras. |
| spect | 5 | Minimal. The spectacle is Bridges' body in motion—every punch costs something. The action is intimate: hotel rooms, safe houses, quiet confrontations. When violence happens, it's brutal and brief. The show trusts performance over pyrotechnics. |
| conc | 7.5 | Atonement and aging. Can you outrun past sins when the past won't stay buried? What loyalty means when institutions betray you. The show explores whether survival is worth the cost of survival. The Afghanistan thread is political history made personal. |
| drive | 8 | Every episode reveals new layer: why Chase ran, what he did, who's coming now. The dual timelines create suspense—past informs present, present explains past. The chase structure provides urgency; the flashbacks provide meaning. The show knows how to end episodes. |
| resol | 7 | Season 1 is complete story with revelations and confrontation. Season 2 continues. The Afghanistan mystery pays off; the present-day chase concludes while opening new doors. FX renewed, so the story continues. The season works as unit. |

| conc | 4 | Prequels answer questions no one asked: "How were Witchers created?" "Where did monsters come from?" The answers don't deepen The Witcher; they make the world smaller. The show is a Wikipedia entry dramatized. |

| spect | 5 | The spectacle is medical—procedures rendered with gruesome authenticity. | conc | 6 | The real-time conceit is the hook: 15 episodes, one hour each, covering one shift. This isn't revolutionary (24 did it), but applied to medicine, it creates unique tension. | drive | 8 | The clock is the villain. Each episode covers one hour of a 15-hour shift, and the format creates relentless momentum. Patients arrive, crises escalate, staffing problems compound. | resol | 7 | Ongoing. The show is still finding itself, but the first season delivers a complete arc: the shift ends, the doctors survive (mostly), the day is done. | resol | 5.5 | Three seasons before cancellation. The mythology builds through all three with ending that could serve as series or season finale. The Gecko boys have arc completion; Santanico gets resolution. Incomplete but not unsatisfying. |

| char | 6.5 | Anthology format means different actors each episode. Quality varies but overall competent. Some episodes feature standout performances; others rely more on concept than character. |
| world | 7.5 | Eight futures: AI-managed democracy, biotech inequality, digital afterlife, post-scarcity economics. Each world is explored in 45 minutes with surprising depth. The interconnection reveals gradually. |
| cine | 7 | Each episode has distinct visual style matching its future. AI-governed society is clean; digital afterlife is saturated. The production values support each concept. |
| spect | 6 | Futures technology realized: AI interfaces, biotech labs, virtual spaces. Budget constraints show but ambition compensates. The spectacle is conceptual as much as visual. |
| conc | 7.5 | Technology and choice. What we gain and lose with each innovation. The episodes don't offer easy answers—I Pluribus asks what "many" means when technology makes us one. |
| drive | 7 | Each episode is complete story. The throughline—how futures connect—builds across the season. Anthology pacing means some episodes drag, others accelerate. |
| resol | 6.5 | Complete season with anthology structure. The final episode reveals connections, but each story stands alone. Philosophical science fiction without forced closure. |
